# P R E F A C E .

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut drop cap 'Q' featuring floral and foliate motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>Although this little work now offered to the Public appears a bit late, if one considers it a Critique of the Principles of Mr. Bekker <note>Balthasar Bekker (1634–1698) was a Dutch theologian who famously argued in his book *The World Enchanted* that the Devil had no power or presence in the physical world, a view that sparked intense controversy.</note>, it arrives in good time if viewed as a historical explanation of the doctrine of the Gods and Demons <note>In the context of "Paganism," the term "Demons" refers to the Greek *daimones*, which were considered intermediate spirits between gods and humans, rather than purely evil entities.</note> of Paganism.

Indeed, the intention here was not so much to refute that Author as to provide a general idea of the sentiments of the Pagans in this regard. And if one deviates from this historical discussion to combat error, it is only in relation to the subjects being treated, in order to resolve all the difficulties that might arise.

There is perhaps no subject that has been treated more diversely than this one; because there is perhaps none that has been meditated upon with less attention.

One does not go to draw from the writings

It was therefore believed necessary to clarify this matter; and to this end, it was necessary to consult the Pagan Authors, and to assert nothing except upon their formal testimonies. If some passages from the Church Fathers <note>The Church Fathers were influential Christian theologians and writers of the first few centuries of the Church, such as Augustine or Origen, who often wrote against pagan practices.</note> have also been extracted, it is because, far from being suspect, they have been found to be entirely convincing.

After having explained the Pagan beliefs <note>The original term "sentimens" refers to the underlying opinions, doctrines, or "way of thinking" of the Greeks and Romans.</note> therein, we proceed to seek the source from which they could have drawn the basis for all these fabulous opinions—regarding their Gods as much as their Demons—and it is found in substance in the Old Testament, from which they borrowed various truths to serve as the subject matter for their mythology <note>This reflects a common 17th-century scholarly theory called "Primal Revelation," which suggested that all pagan myths were corrupted or "fabulous" versions of stories found in the Hebrew Bible.</note>.

It is through this path that we enter into the examination of Mr. Bekker’s system <note>Balthasar Bekker (1634–1698) was a controversial Dutch theologian who, in his work *The World Bewitched*, argued that the Devil and demons had no physical power or influence in the world. His "system" was seen as a threat to traditional views on spiritual warfare.</note>. Here, we focus solely on the argument drawn from the existence of Demons as revealed in the Old Testament and acknowledged by all nations, in order to establish their workings. And by following this path, the objections of Mr. Bekker are resolved.

In this examination, with regard to the Oracles <note>In the ancient world, Oracles were specific sites where deities supposedly spoke to humans. The author is likely debating whether these were mere human trickery or actual demonic interventions.</note> and the specific facts our author alleges concerning the workings of Demons, one appears to show a generosity <note>In this context, "libéralité" means a willingness to grant certain points to the opponent for the sake of the argument.</note> that might perhaps not be approved, if it were not observed that one is

# TABLE

# Principal Subjects

### I LETTER.

General remarks on the system of *Mr. Bekker* <note>Balthasar Bekker (1634–1698) was a Dutch theologian whose book *The World Bewitched* argued that the Devil has no power to interfere in human affairs, a highly controversial stance at the time.</note>, and particularly on his accusation that we make a God out of the Devil. Plan of the work.

### II LETTER.

Coarseness of common Paganism. Degrees of idolatry. That the natural idea of God, however corrupted it may have been among the Pagans, was able to lead them to the knowledge of Him. Sentiments of the principal Philosophers on the existence and properties of God. They mocked the plurality of Gods. Various examples of profanation committed against the Gods and their images. The mysteries of Egypt were the source from which the Greek Philosophers first drew their Gods. How idolatry was established and strengthened. Reasons why the Scholars did not disabuse the common people. What the Philosophers understood by their Gods.

### III LETTER.

Etymologies of the term Demon. What the Demon of Socrates was <note>Socrates famously claimed to have a "daimonion," a divine inner voice that warned him against making mistakes.</note>. Different meanings of these terms: God <note>original Greek: "Θεὸς"</note>, Divine Spirit <note>original Greek: "Δαιμονιον"</note>, and Demons <note>original Greek: "Δαιμονες"</note>. Sentiments

# HISTORICAL TREATISE ON THE GODS AND DEMONS OF PAGANISM,
## With some Critical remarks on the System of Mr. Bekker. <note>Balthasar Bekker (1634–1698) was a Dutch theologian famous for his book *The World Bewitched*, in which he argued that the Devil and demons have no power to interfere in human affairs, a view that sparked immense controversy.</note>
---
### FIRST LETTER.

Sir,

I have deliberately delayed until now to speak with you regarding the System <note>The "System" refers to Bekker's philosophical and theological framework based on Cartesian logic.</note> of Mr. Bekker, because I believed it was necessary to wait for time to restore minds to their natural composure and dispose them to examine things without passion. It is not that I mean to say that this work could have dazzled eyes as piercing as yours; for you are not a man to let yourself be so easily caught off guard. But there are others who, admitting without examination everything that bears the mark of novelty, abandon themselves to it blindly. To wish to bring these people back to reason during the first stirrings of their passion would be to irritate them and expose—

<summary>In this opening letter, the author explains his decision to wait for the initial public controversy surrounding Balthasar Bekker's skeptical "system" to subside before offering a critical historical analysis of pagan gods and demons.</summary>
<keywords>Balthasar Bekker, demonology, paganism, religious skepticism, Cartesianism, 17th-century theology</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues from the previous page's discussion of the public's initial reaction to Balthasar Bekker's skeptical views on demons. The catchword "ser" from page 1 likely connects to a word like "servir" (to serve), though the OCR on this page reads "fer," a common confusion with the archaic "long s" (ſ).</meta>

to their bad mood. It was therefore wise to give them time to come to their senses, and to vent, so to speak, their first fire, before undertaking to disabuse them.

To deny the operations of Demons on earth is a proposition that strikes the mind; one feels a natural inclination to examine such works. The wits <note>French: *beaux esprits*—intellectuals or socialites who prided themselves on modern, skeptical thinking.</note> who wish to distinguish themselves in all things do not fail to make a merit of their incredulity in this regard; and the common people ask for nothing better than to be delivered from these objects of terror. Their views being extremely limited, they imagine that one cannot banish Demons from the world without destroying their existence. Judge after that, if they fall asleep in vice through the hope of impunity. If there are no Devils, there are also no punishments to fear. *The descent to Hell is easy.* <note>original: *Facilis descensus Averni*—a quote from Virgil's *Aeneid*, suggesting that while it is easy to fall into sin or the underworld, returning is the difficulty.</note>

One cannot therefore deny that the subject the Author treats excites curiosity, and that his sentiment finds favorable predispositions in minds. But it must also be confessed that after these first stirrings, one does not fail to return to oneself; the torrent having passed, one examines seriously why one abandoned oneself to it: and if an Author has not supported his sentiment with solid arguments, he has the misfortune of seeing himself abandoned. This is what happened to Mr. Bekker; at first one was

<summary>The author explains how Bekker’s denial of demonic influence appealed to both "wits" seeking novelty and the masses seeking to escape fear, but warns that such skepticism can lead to moral laxity.</summary>
<keywords>Balthasar Bekker, demonology, skepticism, moral philosophy, public opinion, Virgil, Avernus</keywords>

### Of Gods and Demons, etc.

all fire for his first two Books; but one has become all ice for his last two; and his most ardent followers begin to abandon him.

For my part, I read his work more to obey you than to satisfy myself: I found there what I had heard said so many times—much zeal and boldness in putting forward novelties <note>In a 17th-century context, "novelties" (*nouveautés*) often referred to radical or heterodox ideas that strayed from established religious tradition.</note>, but no proof to support them; and if you do not allow yourself to be surprised by a certain air of triumph with which he animates his expressions, you run the risk of remaining always enchanted <note>A play on the title of Bekker's book, *The Enchanted World*. The narrator suggests that Bekker's own rhetorical style acts as a sort of spell on the reader.</note>; particularly, if you deny certain Principles that he lays down there without proof, the whole work will fall of its own accord.

However, the doctrine he refutes is not among those things where the mere proposition carries its own refutation: It is venerable by its antiquity, universal by its belief, and supported by proofs that are at least 
<margin>Book 1, Page 5.</margin>
quite specious <note>Here, "specious" is used in its older sense of "plausible" or "fair-seeming," rather than necessarily "deceptive."</note>; for *the sentiment that the world has regarding the Devil has enough likelihood.* He should therefore have founded his system on good arguments drawn from Scripture and Reason <note>The two standard pillars of theological debate during the Enlightenment: the Bible (Scripture) and logical thinking (Reason).</note>. It is the maxim of all Authors, and particularly of those who advance novelties. A judicious man is not content to read an Author who evaporates into hollow speculations, merely to know what a thing is not. One wants to know what it is positively in itself, to feed on something

# Historical Treatise

solid enough to establish a full certainty in the mind. 

But particularly, it is certain that when it comes to offering new interpretations of Holy Scripture <note>original: "Ecriture Ste"</note>, one must not do so until after supporting them with incontestable proofs drawn from Revelation <note>In this context, "Revelation" refers to the word of God as revealed in the Bible, considered the ultimate authority.</note> itself. One cannot take enough precautions in this regard. These are sacred boundaries that should only be approached with profound veneration; and when one takes such liberties, one should at least do so seriously, and not pepper the explanations given with certain witty remarks which, although to the taste of the common people <note>original: "vulgaire"; referring to the uneducated or non-scholarly public.</note>, are extremely tedious to pious souls and solid minds seeking serious and convincing proofs.

From this it follows that the Author, having established without reason certain Principles that form the basis of the question, succeeds no better in the explanations he gives to the sacred Texts. Here is one proof among an infinity of others that we could cite. For example, what could be simpler than the story of Eve’s seduction by the serpent that we read in Chapter 3 of the Book of Genesis <note>original: "Genése"</note>. If you compare it to the passages of the New Testament <note>original: "N. T."</note> which have a necessary connection to it, it appears that it was the Devil who used the serpent to seduce the Woman. All the Jewish Doctors <note>This refers to historical rabbinical scholars and theologians whose commentaries shaped early biblical understanding.</note> have recognized this truth; and the simplicity of the story does not allow us

<summary>The author continues his critique of Balthasar Bekker, arguing that new biblical interpretations require solemnity and scriptural proof rather than popular wit. He specifically defends the traditional view that the serpent in Genesis was an instrument of the Devil, citing the consensus of Jewish scholars and the New Testament.</summary>
<keywords>Balthasar Bekker, Biblical criticism, Holy Scripture, Book of Genesis, The Fall, Satan, New Testament, Jewish Doctors, religious tradition</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the critique of Balthasar Bekker, specifically addressing his skeptical analysis of the Fall of Man in Genesis. The previous page ended by suggesting that Bekker’s arguments were not [pas] sufficient to provide a better explanation than tradition.</meta>

### Of Gods and Demons, etc.

not to explain it otherwise. However, the Author raises such a great number of difficulties there, that one no longer knows what the Holy Spirit <note>Referring to the divine inspiration traditionally attributed to the biblical authors.</note> intended to say; the serpent said to the Woman—that is to say, according to him, that the serpent said nothing. And the reason for this is that it did not have the necessary organs to form a human voice. Nor could it have been the Devil using the serpent as an instrument for speaking; for besides the fact that one cannot conceive how a spirit can act upon a body, there would still be the same difficulty in knowing how the Devil could have expressed himself in an intelligible manner, since the serpent he would have used would not have had the required faculties for speech. After this fine dissertation, he leaves his reader in a labyrinth of difficulties without giving them the slightest help to escape, and places them under the necessity of saying, *Elijah will come* <note>original: "Elias veniet"; a Latin phrase used proverbially to mean that a mystery is so deep it will only be solved when the prophet Elijah returns before the Last Judgment.</note>. It was neither the serpent nor the Devil who spoke, separately or together; one cannot say it was God, or an Angel, or Adam, or Eve; who was it, then?

I will not say that there is a mystery hidden beneath the odious exaggeration of these difficulties, but I will remark that this proof—that the Devil could not have spoken through the serpent because a spirit cannot naturally act upon a body, and that the serpent lacks the required organs—is something that, while true in Philosophy <term>Philosophy: Here referring to "natural philosophy" or the science of the era, particularly Cartesianism, which struggled to explain how a non-physical mind or spirit could move a physical body.</term>, is entirely

Is the **Devil**, strictly speaking, the absolute author of all the works that people want us to attribute to him? What **Theologian** <note>A scholar of religious doctrine.</note> has ever considered him to be a **First and Independent Cause**? <note>In philosophy, a "First Cause" is a being that acts of its own power without being moved by another; the author argues that only God holds this status.</note>

Do not say that we employ expressions strong enough to give rise to the accusations of **Mr. Bekker** <note>Balthasar Bekker (1634–1698), a Dutch minister whose book "The Enchanted World" argued that the Devil has no power to intervene in the physical world.</note>, or that we give the **Devil** too much authority. Is this not merely the common theme of our **Preachers** to intimidate the wicked? Do our **Theologians** not exaggerate his power so much that they make us conceive of him as a God? He is the cause and the director of storms and tempests; it is he who ignites wars, who causes famine and mortality; he enters into councils and presides there; he suggests evil thoughts to men. Finally, his Empire is so vast and so absolute that it excludes the **Creator**. This appears surprising; but it is, nonetheless, precisely the idea formed by the expressions of our most celebrated **Doctors**. <note>In this context, "Doctors" refers to "Doctors of the Church," highly learned theologians whose writings established official doctrine.</note>

Why do we not say instead that it is God who punishes, that it is he who sends the storms, who afflicts men with war, famine, and mortality; that he alone searches the hearts and minds <note>original: "sonde les reins"; literally "probes the kidneys," a biblical idiom (e.g., Psalm 7:9) referring to God's ability to examine the deepest human thoughts and emotions</note> and hardens hearts, who blinds the eyes of the understanding, and who gives power to error? Allow me, Sir, to ask you as well, why it is said that man moves, that he speaks, that he eats, and that he drinks. This is to speak very improperly; he is only a <term>secondary cause</term> <note>original: "cause seconde"; an agent that produces effects through the power granted by the primary cause (God)</note> who acts only insofar as God imparts to him the virtue necessary to act. For he is in such a state of powerlessness to produce the slightest operation by himself, that God must precede him, move him, and concur in all his actions. Man is therefore only a <term>secondary cause</term> which, when considered in its own nothingness, can do nothing of itself. ...is but an instrument that borrows all its action from God; and I believe I am right to say that one is mistaken to attribute all these operations to man; since by himself he can do nothing. It would then no longer be man who moves, who speaks, who eats, who drinks, but God himself; in the same way that it is not the Devil who produces the works of which we have spoken, but God. This is to rush headlong into a strange consequence; but one cannot avoid it, since it flows necessarily from the same <term>Principle</term> <note>The "Principle" here refers to the theological premise that God is the source of all movement and existence.</note>: For the Devil and man are, in relation to God in this case, one and the same thing, in equal powerlessness, in entire dependence. If one wishes to press this Principle, one will draw monstrous consequences from it.

It is therefore evident that the action must be properly attributed to the agent, particularly when the agent is an <term>intelligent substance</term> <note>In the philosophy of the time, a "substance" is something that exists independently; an "intelligent substance" refers to a being with a mind and will, such as a human, an angel, or a demon.</note>, such as the Devil is. One does not exclude the <term>Primary Cause</term> <note>A term for God, the ultimate origin of all things.</note>; on the contrary, one supposes it, considering its influence as absolutely necessary. But that does not prevent the creature from being the one who acts, and from receiving its <term>denomination</term> <note>original: "dénomination"; the name or identity given to a being based on the specific act it performs (e.g., calling someone a "singer" because they are the one singing).</note> from the action it produces.

From this it follows that we, who are ignorant of all these springs and the way to make them act, are strangely struck at the sight of his operations; we regard as a miracle what is often enough only an operation of the Demon, produced by causes applied and moved differently than according to the ordinary course of nature. Furthermore, I cannot understand why these people wish to find opposition between the **Omnipotence** of **God** and the ministry <note>In this sense, "ministry" refers to the demon acting as an instrument or agent through which God's will (or justice) is carried out.</note> of the **Devil**. Prejudices are a strange thing; they so blind the mind that they render it incapable of maturely examining whether what is put forward might not be retorted: For by this objection which the **Author** makes so often, he completely overthrows his own **hypothesis**. Let us admit here all his explanations and those of his disciples; but let us also be permitted to reason.

# Of Gods & of Demons, etc.

...mysteries of Paganism, errs <note>The French "pêche" literally means "sins," but in this context of intellectual critique, it means "to fail" or "to be in error."</note> in several essential points, but principally: I. In the belief that the most enlightened among the Pagans held regarding the Gods. They did not blindly believe in this multitude of high, middle, and low Divinities, nor did they indifferently attribute supreme power to them. II. It errs in the doctrine of Demons; the Pagans in general never confused them with their Gods; they were, according to them, inferior agents, the Ministers of the Gods, Mediators between them and men, and destined either to help or to harm them, without having invested them with an absolute authority <margin>Book 2. Page 41.</margin> or, not fully understanding the perfection of the Divine Being, *the thought having occurred to them because of this, that God had need of Demons—that is to say, of such spirits in the capacity of Lieutenants—to share among themselves the Government of the world, where the providence of an effeminate Jupiter* <note>Original: "Jupiter efféminé." In 17th-century theological polemics, "effeminate" was often used to describe a deity that was perceived as weak, soft, or lacking the vigorous, all-encompassing power attributed to the Christian God.</note> *could not have extended.* III. It errs in the explanation of Magic and of the various types of Pagan Divination; they did not believe these mysteries to be so sacred that they did not often suspect them of imposture, and openly mocked them. # Historical Treatise

...constant [nature] of the Gods and of the Demons, faithfully explained and cleared of common errors and poetic fictions, must flow from some source; there must have been certain truths that served as their foundation. The discussion of this was not very difficult: Reduce Pagan Theology to its true principles, and you will find that it draws most of its mysteries from Judaism; that its deities, as the ancient philosophers described them, were modeled after the Patriarchs <note>A common 17th-century argument held that pagan myths were corrupted versions of Biblical history—for example, identifying Saturn with Noah or Hercules with Samson.</note>, from whom the Pagans borrowed certain truths which they crudely applied to their false gods; and that their good and evil demons are, in substance, only what they learned from the Jews and from reading the Old Testament <note>Original: "V. T." for *Vieux Testament*.</note>. And so, once these opinions are rectified and unburdened of the fables mixed in over the centuries, they will lead you straight to their origin: to the Jews, who received these truths from God himself, and from whom other nations borrowed them. This path is sure and natural, and if Mr. Bekker <note>Balthasar Bekker (1634–1698), a Dutch theologian whose book *The World Bewitched* argued that since God is all-powerful, spirits and the Devil have no power over the physical world, thus dismissing the reality of witchcraft.</note> had followed it, I doubt he would have continued with his work.

To which must be added one final remark that we shall expand upon further in our last letters; it is that the Author succeeds no better when comparing Pagan superstitions with the sentiments he claims Christians hold regarding the Devil:

<meta>This page continues the critique of Balthasar Bekker’s "The World Bewitched." The previous page ended mid-word with "Devi-", which is completed here as "Devil."</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>Of Gods & of Demons etc.</header>

...[Devil]. For as his goal in describing the opinions of Paganism in his first Book was to make it appear entirely ridiculous—by attributing to it the crudest notions imaginable—so too is the parallel he draws with Christianity completely unjust, which he makes look hideous through his usual accusations.

I admit that most Pagan beliefs were based on fables, yet they were not all entirely false. One should have made this distinction: discarding the fictions to focus on the core and substance of the matter, presenting them faithfully according to the unanimous consensus of the Philosophers; one should also have cleared Christianity of a thousand old wives' tales <note>Original: "contes de vieilles," referring to superstitious folklore or common myths not supported by scripture.</note>, and instead derived its true sentiments from the writings of our theologians.

After such an exact explanation, do not doubt, Sir, that the power of the Devil would have appeared quite limited to you. These rectified Pagan beliefs, far from making you skeptical, would have prepared you to accept this doctrine, seeing that it contains nothing contrary to sound reason. But let us not get ahead of ourselves. We shall see in what follows that Mr. Bekker <note>Balthasar Bekker (1634–1698), whose work argued against the reality of demonic influence.</note>, by constantly avoiding the heart of the matter, builds his argument on false principles and contradicts himself on essential points.

### SECOND LETTER

Sir,

If one limited Pagan Theology to what the Poets relate to us, and to what the common people believed, there would first be cause for astonishment as to how man—who has preserved some traces <note>Original: "linéamens," referring to the faint features or outlines of a divine likeness remaining in human nature.</note> of the image of God and who possesses a natural idea of Him—could have abandoned himself to such crude superstitions. But it must also be acknowledged that not everyone is capable of reflecting on natural notions: once one has been imbued with certain errors, one can almost never be rid of them. Prejudices grow stronger with time and acquire a kind of empire over reason. People so strongly biased <term>prejudiced: in this 17th-century context, "prévenu" implies someone whose mind is already occupied by an opinion before they can judge for themselves</term> Deify the most vile Creatures, without perceiving that what they worship as God is far below the excellence of man.

From this it follows that the Pagans, who had no other guide than the smoking wick <note>A metaphor for a dim or failing light that provides little clarity.</note> of their reason, fell into a kind of delirium by creating as many

Their most venerated Gods, as the Poets depict them for us, were more suited to provoke laughter than to excite devotion. *Euhemerus* <note>A Greek mythographer who argued that the gods were originally mortal kings deified after death—a theory now called "Euhemerism."</note> says in substance that *Saturn* took *Ops* as a wife; that *Titan*, who was the eldest of his children, wished to reign; but that *Vesta* their mother and *Ceres* and *Ops* their sisters advised *Saturn* not to give up the Empire. Seeing this, *Titan*, who felt himself to be the weaker, agreed with *Saturn* on the condition that if he fathered any male children, he would not raise them, so that the Empire would return to his own children. Thus they killed the first son born to *Saturn*; afterwards *Jupiter* and *Juno* were born, of whom they only showed *Juno*, and gave *Jupiter* to *Vesta* to be raised in secret; afterwards came *Neptune*, who was also hidden, and finally *Pluto* and *Glauca*; they showed *Glauca*, who died shortly after, and *Pluto* was raised like *Jupiter* in secret. Now, this having reached the ears of *Titan*, he assembled his children and put *Saturn* and *Ops* in prison. But *Jupiter*, having grown up, fought against the *Titans* and defeated them, and brought his Father and Mother out of prison. However, having discovered that his Father, whom he had restored, was jealous of him and was making attempts on his life, he seized control of the State and banished him to *Italy*.

The Pagans distinguished their Gods into various orders; some were *Greater* <note>original Latin: "Majores"</note> or common, as *Virgil* calls them,

The other Gods were considered intermediate deities—celestial, terrestrial, aquatic, and infernal—to whom the governance of certain parts of the Universe was entrusted. It is not that I claim that Paganism was always so crude: it had its degrees of corruption. The world fell into delirium in proportion to its age. It seems that the Chaldeans <note>Inhabitants of southern Babylonia, known in antiquity for their astrology.</note> & the Sabeans <note>An ancient people of South Arabia, often associated in history with the worship of celestial bodies.</note> after the deluge, even before Abraham who had been raised in their superstition, had as supreme Divinities the Sun, which they worshiped under the names of Baal-Peor, Bel, and Moloch; and the Moon and the Stars: And to represent and make these Divinities favorable to them, they erected images of gold to the Sun and silver to the Moon; they offered them fruits of the earth, apples, wine, oil, and particularly horses to the Sun, as the Persians practiced according to the report of Herodotus <note>A 5th-century BCE Greek historian known as the "Father of History."</note>. As for the Stars, the Egyptians venerated them exceedingly, because they regarded them as so many Intelligences <note>In early modern philosophy and theology, "Intelligences" were spiritual beings or spirits believed to govern the movements of celestial bodies.</note> who influenced every part of the world, and whose observation they claimed would reveal the future and an infinity of secrets.

Besides these Celestial Bodies, which passed among them as the primary Divinities, they also deified earthly things: the Elements—fire, air, water, earth—and subsequently men, their Kings, and generally all those who had procured some benefit for them. Finally, they fell into the most shameful of all idolatries by worshiping not only animals, and particularly the bull *Apis*, upon which it is quite probable the Israelites modeled the golden calf; but also inanimate creatures, such as fruits and herbs <note>This likely refers to the Egyptian practice of venerating plants like leeks and onions, famously satirized by Roman poets like Juvenal.</note>.

# **Of Gods and Demons, etc.**

<margin>Juvenal, *Satires* 15.</margin>
> "It is a sin to violate or break with the teeth a leek or an onion. O holy nations, whose gods are grown in their gardens!" <note>Original Latin: *Porrum & cepe nefas violare & frangere morsu. O sanctas gentes quibus haec nascuntur in hortis, Numina!* The poet Juvenal is here mocking the Egyptians for worshiping garden vegetables.</note>

From the Egyptians, the Greeks borrowed most of their Gods and their mysteries. Everything that came to them from Egypt was Sacred to them. This is what most Philosophers and Historians teach us, particularly Herodotus, Diodorus Siculus, and Plutarch. Consequently, it was believed that there was no true wisdom in Greece unless it had been drawn from Egypt. For this purpose, it was necessary for the Philosophers to travel there to study the mysteries: 

<margin>Plutarch, *On Isis and Osiris*.</margin>
Such were, according to Plutarch, Solon, Thales, Plato, Eudoxus, Pythagoras, and Lycurgus <note>These figures represent the pillars of Greek law, science, and philosophy, all of whom were historically said to have traveled to Egypt to seek ancient knowledge.</note>, who transplanted Egyptian Gods and Ceremonies into Greece.

Now, as there has never been a People more fertile in fictions than the Greeks, they were not satisfied with simply having adopted the Gods of Egypt; they also invented new ones, and attributed to them an infinity of crimes and absurdities <term>impertinences: in this context, it refers to behavior that is silly, trivial, or beneath the dignity of a deity</term>; such were Saturn, Jupiter, Neptune, Pluto, Juno, Venus, etc. Although these originated from Egypt in terms of the actual things re-

<summary>This page explains how Greek religion and philosophy were heavily derived from Egyptian traditions, noting that famous thinkers like Plato and Pythagoras traveled to Egypt to study. It also critiques how the Greeks added their own "fictions" and immoral myths to the original Egyptian deities.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the evolution of idolatry, transitioning from the Greek adoption of Egyptian gods to the Roman assimilation of all foreign deities, concluding with a summary of the hierarchical stages of pagan worship.</meta>

### Traité Historique

...took on a purely Greek form.

Finally, Sir, the Romans, after having established their liberty under the reign of Numa <note>Numa Pompilius was the legendary second king of Rome, credited with establishing many of the city's religious and political institutions.</note>, thought to seek out Gods; it was quite difficult for them to invent new ones, the Egyptians and the Greeks having created almost as many Gods as there were creatures: Thus, they first contented themselves with certain chosen Divinities. But in proportion to their expansion, they increased their number. From this it follows that, as there has never been an Empire so extensive, there has never been one that had so many Gods. Their Pantheon <note>Referring here both to the specific temple in Rome and the collective group of all gods recognized by the state.</note> contained an infinite number of them, so that Rome became the sewer of the idolatry of all ages, and whatever each nation worshiped that was most monstrous was gathered there and served, along with several other extravagant Divinities that the Romans had invented themselves.

Thus you see, Sir, that idolatry has had various degrees. First, they served the Sun as the supreme God, as well as the Moon and the Stars, believing that these celestial bodies were worthy of worship because of their excellence and their utility. Next, the elements and men were deified; and finally, the most vile creatures were venerated. It is particularly this kind of idolatry, which the Greeks and Romans carried to excess, of which I have given you a small sample.

...so that I am not accused of having made Paganism <term>Paganism: A term used by historical Christian writers to describe the polytheistic religions of the ancient world.</term> less ugly than it is in reality.

I admit that if one only stayed at the surface of things, nothing would seem more ridiculous and more opposed to common sense than Paganism. But when one considers that these people, though without Revelation <note>Divine truth revealed by God to humanity, specifically through scripture, as opposed to truth discovered through human reason alone.</note>, were nonetheless humans like us, intelligent and reasonable, it seems that one should suspend judgment until the matter has been examined more closely.

Indeed, when one takes as a general Principle that all men are born with a certain notion of Divinity that we call an "idea"—which is nothing other than an indelible mark that God carves into their understandings, and which reveals His Nature and Perfections to them when they reflect upon it—one cannot believe that they did not sometimes pay attention to it. # Of Gods and Demons, etc.

In his books **On the Nature of the Gods** <note>original: *De Natura Deorum*, a major philosophical work by the Roman orator Cicero (106–43 BC) exploring different theological views.</note>, he states: "There is nothing," he says, "more excellent than God; it must therefore necessarily follow that He governs the world." God neither obeys nor is He subject to Nature; He Himself governs all of Nature. And in defining the Nature of God, he says: "This God whom we conceive cannot be understood in any other way than as a certain Intelligence, detached, free, and separated from all mortal composition, sensing and moving all things."

<margin>Seneca, *On Premature Death*. <note>original: *De immatura morte*</note> Cited in Lactantius, Book <unclear>I</unclear> <note>Lactantius (c. 250–325 AD) was an early Christian author often called the "Christian Cicero."</note></margin>

Seneca teaches this truth in several places in his works; "You do not know," he says, speaking of premature death, "your Author and the Majesty of your Judge, the Governor of the earth and the Heavens, the God of gods, upon whom these deities we worship depend." <note>original: "ont été dispensées." This implies that the lesser gods are merely administrators or derivatives of the one true God.</note>

From this, it follows that the <term>Church Fathers: Ancient and influential Christian theologians whose writings established the intellectual foundations of the faith.</term> Church Fathers did not fail to turn the Pagans' own sentiments regarding Divinity back upon them. They did this to convince them that these other gods they had forged were repugnant to their own ideas of the Unity, Power, and <term>Providence: The belief in God's ongoing intervention and guidance of the universe.</term> Providence of God. 

<margin>Tertullian, *Apology*, Chapter 24 <note>Tertullian (c. 155–220 AD) was a prolific early Christian author from Carthage.</note></margin>

"Even if we were to grant you," says Tertullian, "that your gods were true deities, would you not admit, according to common opinion, that there is a God greater and more powerful than they? One who is, as it were, the Prince and Author of the Universe, endowed with a..."

Apply these ideas of Seneca to Jupiter, Sir; and you will find that he allows this Supreme God to be conceived as a poor old man, only too happy to enjoy Roman immunities.

Plutarch, in countless places, treats the tales told about the Gods as fiction, and particularly in his *Treatise on the Oracles* where he has Cleombrotus say: *“It is a mockery, my friend, to say that Apollo, for having killed the Dragon <note>referring to the Python of Delphi</note>, was forced to flee to the ends of Greece to be rehabilitated and purified, and that there he made certain offerings and libations, as men do when they wish to appease the wrath of the Demons whom we call Alastoras and Palamneos <note>Greek terms for avenging spirits or deities who punish crimes</note>, that is to say, those who pursue the punishment and vengeance of crimes, the memory of which lasts forever.”* In this same treatise, he considers the Jupiter of Homer as a pure fiction; for after having said that the Gods are entirely free, without anyone...

Let us stop, Sir, at these few passages; for we would never be finished if we undertook to extract the opinions of all the Pagan Authors on this matter. You may conclude from those I have just cited what their veneration for their Gods truly was. There is a certain pleasure in seeing them insult their own deities. If they showed no mercy to *Jupiter*, how much liberty must they have taken when speaking of the other Divinities?

Plutarch, in the Book cited above <note>referring to *On Isis and Osiris* cited on page 61</note>, after having explained several hieroglyphic figures of the Egyptians, warns the person he is addressing that when he hears tell of such fictions, he should remember what has been said and believe that they do not mean that anything of the sort ever existed in reality. For they do not mean—this is an example he cites—that Mercury <note>The author likely refers to the Egyptian god Anubis, whom the Greeks and Romans associated with Hermes/Mercury (the "Hermanubis").</note> is literally a Dog, but rather the nature of this animal, which is to guard, to be vigilant, wise in seeking, in knowing, and in discerning friend from foe. It is thus, he adds, that by making this discernment, and by studying to have a sound and true opinion of the Gods, you will avoid superstition, which is no less a sin than the impiety of not believing there are Gods at all.

<summary>This page explains that Egyptian animal worship and hieroglyphs were symbolic allegories for divine attributes rather than literal idols. Citing ancient historians, the author argues that these symbols were intended to lead the wise toward a true understanding of the divine while avoiding the twin traps of superstition and atheism.</summary>

# Of Gods and Demons, etc.
<meta>catchword: in</meta>

...finish. The Greeks, a curious people and great enthusiasts of the mysteries of Egypt, having learned them there through their dealings with the Priests and Philosophers, returned home with their minds full of an enigmatic Theology, of hieroglyphic figures, and of mysterious Divinities; in short, of a Religion quite different from how it appeared on the surface. Not even their words and explanations escaped contracting this Egyptian obscurity. 

Pythagoras, for example, who was a Disciple of Oenuphis of Heliopolis <note>original: "Oenupheus d'Heliopolis." Oenuphis was an Egyptian priest mentioned by Plutarch as the person who taught Pythagoras the secrets of the Egyptian faith; Heliopolis was the "City of the Sun," a major religious center.</note>, derived his Philosophy from that of Egypt, and hid <margin>Plutarch, *On Isis and Osiris*.</margin> his doctrine under figurative and enigmatic words. *Do not eat on a stool,* *do not stir the fire with a sword in the house,* etc. <note>These are "Pythagorean symbols" (akousmata). They were practical-sounding rules that held deeper moral meanings; for instance, "not stirring the fire with a sword" was a metaphor for not provoking an already angry person.</note> All of these were so many axioms of his Philosophy that he had learned in Egypt.

Now, you are well aware, Sir, what veneration the Greeks had for their Philosophers, and particularly for those who were versed in the mysteries of Egypt. They took pride in conforming to their opinions; they were made the preceptors of the youth, and the arbiters of the choice of Gods and their Ceremonies. Finally, their influence was so general that the Laws had no power without their approval. Judge by this how easy it was for them to introduce

The others, who were predisposed to venerate their Sages and Leaders, calmly left the care of Religion to them; they knew nothing except what was presented under the mask of Fables. And since the mysteries hidden behind these veils were never revealed to them, they focused themselves with humility on certain outward appearances which seemed venerable to them simply because they did not understand them. It was, therefore, this ignorance of the meaning of their mysteries that led them to take for real Divinities what the Philosophers, who grasped their true sense, regarded merely as emblems.

This is something the Egyptians apparently took from the O.T. <note>V.T. = *Vieux Testament*, or Old Testament.</note> where the name of God, that is to say His nature, is called hidden and ineffable <note>original: *inénarrable*, meaning beyond description or unutterable.</note>. From this comes, to say it in passing, that this God *Amun* or the *hidden one* of the Egyptians and the Greeks, is the same for whom St. Paul found an Altar in Athens upon which was written, *to the unknown God* <note>A reference to the Biblical account in Acts 17:23.</note>. Be that as it may, this emblem was made into a God called *Jupiter Hammon*; the people serve him with extreme devotion, making him the Father of Gods and men, the thunderer, etc. But do you think that enlightened persons gave in to this superstition? Not at all. They knew very well that *Jupiter* had been a King of Crete <note>This reflects "Euhemerism," a theory that Greek myths originated from real historical people and events.</note>, and that *Amun* was a hieroglyph <term>Hieroglyph: Used here to mean a sacred symbol or allegorical image rather than the script itself.</term> come from Egypt; and thus they distinguished what the people confused.

### Of the Gods and the Demons, etc.

To this reflection, I add a second, quite natural one, to show how idolatry became established. One need only reflect a little on the respect held for antiquity, especially regarding Religion. What was a fable a thousand years ago has taken hold in the mind as a sacred thing. The Egyptian mysteries <term>mysteries: secret religious rites or doctrines accessible only to initiates</term>, which were shrouded in an infinite number of hieroglyphs to make them more venerable, might have been understood by the Greeks for a century or two. But they lost the understanding of them in proportion as time distanced them. Nevertheless, they religiously preserved the veils; they retained the exterior, which they loaded with new fictions.

Fumée <note>Martin Fumée (c. 1540–1611), a French writer and translator known for his historical works.</note>, who gave us the translation of Athenagoras <note>Athenagoras of Athens (c. 133–190 AD) was an early Christian philosopher. The priests mentioned here are portrayed as keepers of hidden monotheistic truths.</note> say that there is only one God. Wishing to represent His essence to the simple-minded, each nation invented various images, all of which express but one and the same thing. Their true meaning having been lost over time, the common people <term>vulgar: from the Latin 'vulgus', referring to the common people or the uneducated masses</term> believed there were as many Gods as they saw [images]...

...[an enemy] of images; and that from this source idolatry arose. That *Bacchus*, in building the Temple of *Hammon*, placed no other images there but those of God; because just as there is only one Heaven which contains but one world, so there is in this world only one God who communicates in spirit. Mr. *Huet* <note>Pierre-Daniel Huet (1630–1721), a French bishop and scholar mentioned on the previous page, known for his work on the origins of myths.</note> adds that certain Egyptian merchants say the same, and more: namely, that the Gods of *Mythology* <term>Fable: In 17th-century usage, "the Fable" referred to the entire body of classical Greek and Roman myths.</term> signify the various actions of this Sovereign and unique Divinity, who is without beginning and without end.

Plutarch <note>Plutarch (c. 46–119 AD) was a Greek philosopher whose work "On Isis and Osiris" is a primary source for the allegorical interpretation of Egyptian religion.</note> vigorously censures this abuse:
> "Just as we say," he says, "that he who buys the Books of *Plato* buys *Plato*, and he who performs the *Comedies* of *Menander* <note>Menander (c. 342–290 BC) was a Greek dramatist and the best-known representative of Athenian New Comedy.</note> performs *Menander*; so too did they call their gifts and inventions by the names of the Gods. But their posterity, taking this crudely and applying it ignorantly, attributed the qualities of the fruits <note>Original: "les accidens de leurs fruits" — referring to physical crops like grain or wine being confused with the deities Ceres or Bacchus.</note> to the Gods themselves. Not only did they call the presence of crops the birth of the Gods, and their absence their death, but they actually believed it to be so. Consequently, they filled themselves with many wicked and confused opinions regarding the Gods, even though the absurdity of their views was plain to see."

It uses Plutarch's critique to show how literal-mindedness transformed philosophical symbols into superstitious religion.</summary>

### Of Gods and Demons, etc.

One must not forget to make a third observation: it is that the **Poets**, through their **fables** <term>fables: In this context, the author refers to the body of mythological stories from classical antiquity.</term>, contributed significantly to plunging the **People** into **superstition**. Since they excelled in the art of lying agreeably, they seduced minds through the genealogy, the titles, and the duties of the **Gods**. **Homer**, who had visited the **Egyptians**, drew from their parabolic fictions <note>The author reflects a common early modern belief that Greek mythology was a derivative, "veiled" version of older Egyptian wisdom.</note> that ingenious Romance which has been the admiration of all antiquity. His manner of immortalizing his **Heroes** appeared so pleasing and touched the curious minds of the **Greeks** so deeply that, over time and contrary to his intention, they took his fables for truths. However, the **scholars** respected them because, through these veils, they perceived certain truths that were ingeniously shaded; meanwhile, the common people, stopping at the surface <note>Original: "l'écorce" (the bark or rind). This is a metaphor for the literal, outward meaning of a story versus its internal, allegorical truth.</note>, abused them crudely.

But, you will say, why did the **Scholars** not correct this abuse? It is not difficult, Sir, to answer you. I believe I have suggested that their vanity was the cause. They were creatures of glory <term>animaux de gloire: A 17th-century idiom for people driven by a desire for fame and intellectual prestige.</term> who, claiming to be the sole guardians of all truths, kept them locked within their own hearts; and if they happened to publish them, they did so in such obscure terms that no one could penetrate their meaning. And thus, they believed that there was no more effective means of winning the veneration of the common people than to hide their enlightenment from them. If they sometimes let a few sparks escape, it was only to dazzle them, and not at all to instruct them. It was necessary to bait the **People** with some outward show; but they were careful not to display their mysteries to their eyes; that would have converted the respect one held for them into contempt.

# Of Gods and Demons, etc.

*...that it was necessary to accustom the common people to observe through these [rituals] all the Edicts of the Princes; and to test, through the observation of these known things, what they thought of hidden matters; for it might happen that those who despised the former would also despise greater things.* <note>The author concludes the thought from Isocrates, suggesting that religious rituals served as a "litmus test" for political loyalty; if a citizen obeyed a small religious rule, they were likely to obey the laws of the state.</note>

*Now, it was quite difficult to keep the Peoples in respect through other motives. Their crude conceptions of the Divinity were so deeply rooted that to try to disabuse them would have been to alienate them. Such a design would not have failed to cause upheavals in the States. What need was there, then, to expose oneself by trying to rectify their notions? It was far better to profit from their dispositions, to applaud them in their errors, and to make them believe that these Gods they had forged had been the founders of their Empire, their Republic, their City, and their Legislators; and that their Sovereigns had been sired by them.* <note>This refers to the common ancient practice of "apotheosis" or "divine right," where rulers claimed to be the literal descendants of gods to secure their authority.</note> *This was the way to captivate them with good grace. You want Gods such as you have imagined them? Well then! Follow your inclination, O Peoples: this "Heaven" of the Human Race is an effective bridle to restrain you, since you yourselves have forged and taken it up.* <note>The "bridle" is a metaphor for self-imposed social control; the people are controlled by the very superstitions they created.</note>

*I must conclude, Sir. But I cannot do so without noting two things for*

### Of Gods and Demons, etc.

...do as others do, and that is enough.

But, in order to answer your objection, it is worth observing first that although the learned men of <term>Paganism</term> <note>The various polytheistic religions of the ancient Greco-Roman world.</note> made use of certain expressions consecrated by custom, they nevertheless gave them a meaning very different from that of the <term>vulgar</term> <note>In this historical context, "vulgar" refers to the common people or the uneducated masses, rather than something offensive.</note>. **Jupiter**, **Neptune**, **Pluto**, **Juno**, **Minerva**, and **Ceres** passed among the people for so many Divinities, and in this capacity, a great number of purely external ceremonies had been instituted in their honor, as these were better suited to dazzle and fix the people's attention. 

But the <term>Philosophers</term> and the <term>Politicians</term> <note>Here, "Politicians" refers to those involved in the governance and civil order of the state, who maintained religion for the sake of social stability.</note> found the means to confirm the people in their belief by using the same names, while distinguishing themselves from common opinions by conceiving of these Gods as so many **emblems** either of the Power of God, or of the blessings He granted them. And thus, both groups agreed well enough that it was necessary to serve the Gods, but they did so differently. The people served them by attaching themselves superstitiously to the emblems; but the learned men, who knew their origin and the intention of the <term>Legislators</term> <note>The ancient law-givers who established the religious and social frameworks of their cities.</note>, traced them back to the things signified, or at most rendered them only an **inferior and relative worship** <note>A form of worship where the honor is directed toward what the symbol represents (a higher power) rather than the symbol itself.</note>.

<term>Cicero</term> <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), a Roman statesman and philosopher who wrote extensively on theology and ethics.</note>, after having condemned poetic fictions, adds: "but while despising and rejecting these fables, let us say that God extends through the nature of each thing; thus, they could understand <term>Ceres</term> to mean the earth, <term>Neptune</term> to mean the sea," and 
<margin>Book 1 of *On the Nature of the Gods*</margin>
likewise for the others. This is the same thing he observes elsewhere, saying that the air is what we call **Jupiter**, that the air flowing through the sea is **Neptune**, and that the earth is named **Ceres**.

<margin>Plutarch, *On Isis and Osiris*</margin>
<term>Plutarch</term> <note>A Greek philosopher and biographer (c. 46–120 CE) who sought to reconcile various religious traditions through philosophy.</note> confesses that all the names of the Gods are nothing but pure <term>allegories</term> <note>Stories or characters used as symbols to represent deeper spiritual or natural truths.</note>. The Greeks, he says, allegorize that **Saturn** is Time, that **Juno** is the air, and that the birth of **Vulcan** is the changing of air into fire. Similarly, the Egyptians understand **Osiris** to be the Nile which mingles with **Isis**—that is to say, with the earth; and **Typhon** to be the sea, into which the Nile enters and is lost. Throughout this entire treatise, he explains the significance of the Egyptian Gods.

From this, Sir, it is easy to judge why they served these Divinities; it is because, through these various names, they intended to honor God in every part of the Universe where they noticed the works of His <term>Providence</term> <note>The protective care of God or nature as a spiritual power.</note>, which is always active in providing them with His blessings. **Hercules**, because his strength is invincible, etc. **Mercury**, because he is the author of numbers, order, and science. Wherever you turn, you will encounter him everywhere. Likewise, whether you call God **Nature**, or **Fortune**, these are but the names of the same God who makes use of his power in various ways.

In the second place, I observe that the Pagans in general submitted all their divinities to a single God; having attributed to them only a dependent power and ministerial functions. In Rome, they served a <term>Jupiter the Best and Greatest</term> <note>original: "Jupiter Opt. Max." <margin>Quintus Curtius, Book 8.</margin>
This prejudice having become deeply rooted, dying or becoming a God were the same thing among Princes; "From what I can see," said Vespasian as he was dying, "I am about to become a God." <note>Vespasian's famous last words were recorded in Latin as *Vae, puto deus fio* (Woe, I think I am becoming a god), a deathbed joke mocking the Roman custom of imperial deification.</note> Sometimes even, Princes wanted to be recognized as Gods during their lifetime. This is what happened to Alexander the Great: for having had himself proclaimed as such by Cleon, according to the Persian custom, Callisthenes <note>Callisthenes was a historian and the great-nephew of Aristotle who accompanied Alexander on his Asiatic expedition.</note> could not help but answer him with his usual frankness, *“Truly, it is quite something for you or me, Cleon, to make Gods! I am of the opinion that the King should hold his Divinity only by our votes. But let us test your Power a little; let us see if you can make a King, since you make a God so well; for you will admit to me that it is easier to make the one than the other.”* These were words which cost him his life a short time later, though under another pretext.

Now, as soon as the Prince had been placed among the number of the Gods, his likenesses <term>Simulachres: from the Latin 'simulacrum', referring to statues or cult images intended to represent the presence of a deity.</term> were multiplied, Divine honors were rendered to him, vows and prayers were addressed to him, Temples and Altars were consecrated to him, Priests were given to him, Sacrifices were offered to him, and Feast days were instituted in his honor.

### Of Gods and Demons, etc.

Do not believe, however, that no matter how pompous this exterior was, these new Gods were at first greatly venerated; entire centuries had to pass to give them more credit and veneration; and after all, their power was not unlimited. To them were distributed, as to **Consuls** <note>The author compares the distribution of divine duties to the Roman political system, where Consuls held power over specific provinces or terms.</note>, only certain parts of the world to govern; and even their influences were quite often limited within the boundaries of certain Cities that had chosen some of them to be their **tutelary Gods** <term>Tutelary: From the Latin 'tutularius', referring to a deity serving as a guardian, patron, or protector of a particular place, person, or lineage.</term>.

Besides this, the **Pagans** had attributed to each of them in particular different virtues; one had a virtue that another did not.

And thus, if one wishes to give the Gods of the **Pagans** a meaning that accords with what their Writers teach us about them, it would be necessary to subject them all to a single Being, from whom they borrowed their authority, which they exercised in the capacity of His primary Officers.

The **Learned** were not unaware that their Gods had been men: It was even among them like a problem that could be innocently debated, whether **Jupiter** <note>The Roman King of the Gods. This skeptical approach—viewing gods as historical people whose stories were exaggerated—is known as Euhemerism.</note> had ever existed. ### THIRD LETTER

Sir,

As useless Prefaces are not to your taste, after having entertained you in my previous letter regarding the Gods of **Paganism**, I move on to speak to you of their **Demons** <note>original: "Demons." # THIRD LETTER

## SIR,

As useless Prefaces are not to your taste, after having discussed the Gods of **Paganism** with you in my previous letter, I move on to speak to you of their **Demons** <term>Demons: Derived from the Greek *daimon*, this term refers to intermediary spirits—beings positioned between the divine and the human—rather than exclusively "evil" spirits in the modern sense.</term>. ### Of Gods and Demons, etc.

Now, he intentionally calls God a "Demon"; because the Athenians, whose errors he wished to correct, called all their Gods "Demons." They had filled their entire city with them. They were nothing but stones, or columns that they called <term>pillars</term> <note>original: *πάγοι* (pagoi). While literally meaning "hills" or "crags," the author uses it here to describe the physical monuments or sacred stones ubiquitous in ancient Athens.</note>, temples, altars, victims, or oracles. They placed the Republic under their protection; they gave them direction over all affairs; in short, they believed that without their influence, nothing could exist or prosper.

"Poor people," Socrates said to them then, "how I pity you for serving such Gods, and for attributing such power to Demons who exist only in your imagination! Believe me, rid yourselves of this empty fear, break these images, and purify your worship from this crude superstition; I want to show you that there is only one almighty Demon who alone deserves your adoration. You must believe me, for all that I tell you does not come from me, but from him himself, who communicates familiarly with me."

<margin>Xenophon, *Apology of Socrates*.</margin>

# Of Gods and Demons, etc.

...confused in relation to the Gods, without adding some corrective, or some expression which indicated that the name of "God" was given to them only in an <term>improper manner <note>In this context, it means using a word figuratively or loosely rather than in its strict, literal sense.</note></term>. This makes it sufficiently clear that they conceived of Demons only as Beings subject to the Gods. I do not dwell on this reflection, because we shall expand upon it further in the following pages.

The Pagans in general have indeed recognized that Demons were of a spiritual nature, although less pure and less perfect than that of the Gods. But as this term "spiritual" is a vague idea which signifies nothing unless one explains what it consists of, it is well to observe that there has been almost no error older or more general than that of the gross ideas under which the nature of spirits has been conceived; and one might say that this was the origin of all other errors.

Once one has established as a Principle that Spirits are substances composed of a <term>subtle matter <note>A common historical theory that spirits were not entirely immaterial, but made of an extremely fine, vaporous, or "subtle" form of matter, such as air or fire.</note></term>, one attributes to them its properties and <term>accidents <note>In traditional philosophy, "accidents" are characteristics or qualities of a thing (like its color or position) that are not essential to its nature.</note></term>; one infers that they wish to be served in a manner proportioned to their nature; one multiplies them to infinity, because one easily conceives that it is

### Of Gods and Demons, etc.

Philo the Jew, *On the Giants*. <note>original: "Philo Jud de Gigant." Referring to Philo of Alexandria, a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize the Torah with Greek philosophy.</note>

One perceives clearly enough through the Platonic allegories of <term>Philo <note>Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE), whose works often interpreted biblical stories as philosophical allegories.</note></term> that he held nearly the same sentiment. This was also the belief of the author of the *Books of Enoch*, of which <term>Joseph Scaliger <note>A 16th-century French religious scholar and one of the founders of modern historical chronology.</note></term> inserted several fragments into his notes on <term>Eusebius <note>A 4th-century bishop and historian known as the "Father of Church History."</note></term>. Several of the <term>Church Fathers <note>The influential early Christian theologians and writers who established the intellectual foundations of Christianity.</note></term> also erred regarding the nature of demons. They believed that by making use of the philosophy of <term>Plato <note>The ancient Greek philosopher; early Christians often saw his "Forms" and ideas about the soul as a bridge to Christian doctrine.</note></term>—in which they confusedly glimpsed some truths he had drawn from the Books of Moses—they would combat idolatry with greater success; for this philosopher had emerged from their own midst, and the Pagans held him in great veneration. And as Jewish tradition and Paganism (which was partly formed from the fictions of the Jews) agreed on the materiality of demons, and since the limited knowledge several of the Fathers had of the <term>Holy Language <note>Hebrew.</note></term> was not sufficient to open their understanding to this passage from the Book of Genesis, they were therefore unable to correct this prejudice, which appeared venerable to them due to its antiquity and was supported unanimously by both Jews and Pagans.

These Fathers, being thus so strongly biased, believed that demons had been fathered by angels who married

# Historical Treatise

It is impossible that a spirit of this nature, being subject to bodily relations, could be equally present everywhere, nor consequently govern all parts of the Universe; thus one forms for oneself a sensual happiness, purely bodily pains, Elysian Fields, and a Tartarus <note>The Elysian Fields and Tartarus were the respective "heaven" and "hell" of Greek mythology.</note>; one imagines with <term>Pythagoras <note>An ancient Greek philosopher (c. 570 – 495 BCE) who taught the "transmigration of souls."</note></term> a <term>metempsychosis <note>The philosophical term for the migration of a soul into a new body, whether human, animal, or inanimate, after death.</note></term>, by subjecting the soul to the various changes of matter; and one infers from it with <term>Epicurus <note>A Greek philosopher (341–270 BCE) who argued that the soul is composed of atoms and dissolves at death, meaning there is no afterlife.</note></term> its entire dissolution; if it is a fire, it will be extinguished; if it is a subtle matter, it will dissipate; if it is a harmony, it will be corrupted.

In particular, Demons have almost always been conceived under material ideas. Most Jewish <term>Doctors <note>In this context, "Doctors" refers to the learned Rabbis or scholars of Jewish law and theology.</note></term> fell into this error, which takes its origin from this passage of the Book of Genesis <margin>Genesis 6:2.</margin> which has been misunderstood, where it speaks of the sons of God who took the daughters of men as wives; understanding by the "sons of God," <margin>Josephus, *Jewish Antiquities*, Book 1, Chapter 3.</margin> the Angels who had communication with the daughters of men. Thus they drew upon themselves, says <term>Josephus <note>Flavius Josephus (c. 37–100 CE), a Romano-Jewish historian whose works provide crucial insights into first-century Judaism.</note></term>, *the wrath of God, and the Angels of God who married women produced a race of insolent people, who, through the confidence they had in their own strength, made it their glory to trample Justice underfoot, and imitated those Giants spoken of by the Greeks.*

### Historical Treatise on the Soul and the Demon.

**E 2**

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Obsolescence of Oracles*.</margin>
This is enough to dwell on the belief of the <term>Church Fathers<note>The influential theologians and writers of the early Christian Church.</note></term>. I observe that this sentiment regarding the corporeal nature of Demons was general among the Pagans, although it stemmed from a different Principle. *Hesiod* and some Philosophers who, according to *Plutarch*, distinguished the *first four kinds of rational natures*, felt obliged, in order to form a proper System, to give Demons a nature less spiritual than that of the Gods, but more perfect than that of souls. For they believed that the <term>intelligible world<note>In Platonic philosophy, the "intelligible world" refers to the realm of reality that can only be understood by the intellect, as opposed to the physical world we see around us.</note></term> was composed of four substances that followed one another as if by degrees; and that a transformation occurred from the first to the second, continuing to the fourth nature, which was that of the Gods. This was the highest degree, the final point of these various changes: of good souls into <term>semi-Gods<note>Often referred to in Greek mythology as heroes; beings of divine descent or humans elevated after death.</note></term>; of semi-Gods into Demons; and of Demons—though rarely and after a very great number of centuries—into Gods. Thus, the nature of Demons, which was superior to souls and semi-Gods, and inferior to the Gods, held a sort of middle ground between these Beings.

<margin>Plutarch, ibid.</margin>

# Of Gods and Demons, etc.

...testimonies, both evident and very ancient, that there are neutral and intermediary Natures that stand, as it were, at the extremities of humanity, subject to mortal passions and to receiving necessary changes and variations; and it is these natures that it is reasonable for us to call <term>Demons<note>From the Greek 'daimon'; in this context, referring to intermediary spirits or guiding entities rather than purely evil beings.</note></term>, and that we should honor them, following the tradition and examples of our predecessors.

If you then ask me the cause of this <term>fiction<note>The author uses "fiction" here in the sense of a constructed philosophical or mythological system, rather than necessarily an intentional lie.</note></term>, it will not be difficult to satisfy you. The ancient Pagans, who had learned of the existence and operations of Demons from the <term>Jews<note>A common claim among early Christian apologists was 'Interpretatio Christiana', the belief that all truth found in Greek philosophy was originally plagiarized or inherited from Hebrew scripture.</note></term>—as we shall prove elsewhere—made this doctrine one of the principal points of their Philosophy, according to their general maxim of accommodating all things to their prejudices, and subjected it to their general Principles.

The most universal of all was that of the transformation of bodies into others more excellent by a kind of <term>gradation<note>The process of advancing step-by-step through a series of stages; here, the refinement of the soul or substance.</note></term>. They claimed that each body, after having been clothed for some time in a certain form, took another more refined one; this other form then gave way to a third, more subtle, and so on. It is again *Plutarch* who leads us to this reflection; and we attach ourselves particularly to his Writings because he reports therein...

reports the sentiments of the most famous Philosophers. Others say—these are his words—that a change occurs in bodies as well as in souls, in the same manner that we see water is engendered from earth, air is engendered from water, and fire from air; nature and substance always tending from low to high. And it is by this excellent argument that he proves that souls change into <term>demi-gods<note>In Greek mythology and philosophy, 'demi-gods' (heroës) were seen as a rank above mortals but below full divinity.</note></term>, demi-gods into <term>Demons<note>From the Greek 'daimon'; here referring to spirits that act as intermediaries, not necessarily evil entities.</note></term>, and Demons into Gods.

The nature of Demons being like a step that closely touched that of God and was not distant from that of man, would you find it strange that they were made into so many Mediators between the Gods and men? <margin>Plato, *Laws*, Book 4.</margin>

### Of Gods and Demons, etc.

Chaldeans and the Persians; which the **Manicheans** <note>A religious movement founded by the prophet Mani in the 3rd century AD, known for its "dualist" view of the universe as a struggle between light and darkness.</note> adopted.

Based on this Principle, they divided Nature into two Classes. **Oromasés** <note>original: "Oromasés"; the Greek rendering of Ahura Mazda, the supreme deity of Zoroastrianism representing light and order.</note>, for example, was the Father and the director of virtuous people; everything good in the elements, the animals, and the plants was attributed to him; he bestowed light and summer; in short, he made all of Nature fertile. **Arimianus** <note>original: "Arimianus"; the Greek rendering of Ahriman (Angra Mainyu), the destructive spirit in Zoroastrianism.</note>, on the contrary, was a God whose influences were malignant; he corrupted the Human Race; he afflicted it with an infinity of scourges; he was the author of darkness, of winter, of cold—in a word, of all the disorders that happened in the world.

Nevertheless, these two Principles were not equally esteemed. **Oromasés**, as the author of good, was more excellent; and **Arimianus**, as the author of evil, was less so. They both indeed had absolute authority, each within his own realm <note>original: "ressort"; here meaning a sphere of influence or jurisdiction.</note>; but this difference came from the nature of the things over which their Government was attributed.

It is quite probable that this opinion contributed in no small way to making the Pagans distinguish between **Demons** as being either good or bad. At the very least, this was the opinion of the **Chaldeans**, who had learned from their **Zoroaster** <note>The ancient Iranian prophet who founded Zoroastrianism.</note>, one of the principal authors of the...

two Principles: that the good Demons had bodies composed of light, and the bad ones of darkness.

The good Principle—or, if you prefer, the good Gods—were assigned benevolent **Genies** <note>original: "Genies"; in ancient Roman and Greek belief, a "genius" or "daemon" was a tutelary spirit that guided a person or place, not necessarily an evil entity.</note>, and the bad Principle was assigned malevolent Genies. And as the esteem and veneration held for the good Gods was higher and more voluntary than that shown to the bad ones (whom people feared more than they loved), more perfection was also attributed to the good Demons than to the bad ones.

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Cessation of Oracles*.</margin>
The duties of the Demons were therefore very different. The good Demons were *the spies of the Gods, traveling in every direction, observing and presiding over the Sacrifices and sacred Ceremonies.* The bad ones avenged and punished the outrages, crimes, and injustices of men. **Plutarch** <note>A famous Greek historian and philosopher (c. 46 – 120 AD) who wrote extensively on religion and ethics.</note> compares the nature of the latter to that of men; claiming that there remains in them much filth and some excrement, and that they feed on **suffumigations** <note>The practice of burning incense or other substances so that the smoke may rise as an offering.</note>, blood, and the fat of Sacrifices; in contrast to the good Demons, who are of a purer nature.

He goes further; for he claims that their malignity extends even to defiling sacred Ceremonies. *Furthermore*, he says, *as for what concerns certain cruel and somber Festivals and Sacrifices, such as the ominous*

# Historical Treatise

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Oracles that have ceased* <note>original: "Plutar. des Oracles qui ont ceſſé"; referencing Plutarch's *De defectu oraculorum*, a dialogue concerning why the ancient prophetic centers were losing their power.</note></margin>sentiment held in his time regarding the power of Demons. He introduces Heracleon speaking thus: *“It is not Gods who preside over the Oracles, since it is right to believe that they do not involve themselves in earthly things, but they are rather Demons, the ministers of the Gods.”* In the same treatise, he reports the sentiment of a foreigner which he approves of. *“And if we give,”* he says, *“the names of the Gods to some of these Demons, it should not be a cause for astonishment, said this foreigner; for they are very pleased to be called by the names of the Gods upon whom they depend, and from whom their honor and power derive.”* And a few lines lower; *“but most have the Names of the Gods, which do not suit them at all.”* Elsewhere, wanting to find a middle ground to explain what consists of the nature of Typhon <note>In Greek mythology, Typhon was a monstrous giant; here he is discussed as a "demon" rather than a true deity.</note>, 
<margin>*On Isis and Osiris* <note>original: "De Iſid. & Oſirid."; one of Plutarch's most famous works comparing Egyptian and Greek religious philosophy.</note></margin>of Isis and of Osiris, he agrees with Pythagoras, Plato, Xenocrates, and Chrysippus <note>A list of heavyweight ancient philosophers—ranging from the mathematical Pythagoras to the Stoic Chrysippus—used here to lend authority to the author's argument.</note>, that those writers have done better who have written that what is told of Typhon was not accidents that befell the Gods or men, but rather some great Demons; following the opinion of the ancient Theologians, who believe that they were stronger and more robust than men, and that they surpassed our nature in power, but that they had neither the purity nor the power of the Gods.
