Why do the demons not fear the gods, or why can the gods not make the demons flee? Look at a possessed man: he raves, he lashes out, he is raging. Let us lead him to the Temple of Jupiter; but, because Jupiter would not know how to heal men, let us take him to that of Aesculapius <note>Aesculapius (Asklepios) was the Greek and Roman god of medicine and healing; his temples often served as hospitals where the sick sought "incubation" or divine dreams to cure them.</note> or Apollo; let the priests exorcise him, each in the name of his own god, so that this wicked spirit departs; this can never be done. What then is the power of your gods, if the demons are not subject to them? 

And a little later: "Now, these are nevertheless the very same demons who are detestable to them [the gods]." Truly, would there be the slightest sense in all these passages? Would the pagans not have vigorously retorted <note>The author is suggesting that if the Christians' arguments were true, the pagans could have easily turned those same arguments back against them to prove the power of their own supernatural system.</note> with these arguments to uphold the divinity of their mysteries, if the demons had been for them, at that time, such sacred and all-powerful beings?

I shall say no more on this. It remains for me to speak to you of the mysteries of the pagans. But beforehand, you will permit me to observe, Sir, that although the Greeks and Romans attributed their institution—some to demons and others to gods—they nevertheless agreed on the substance of the matter.

It is certain that the Greeks followed their system well by having demons preside over all the mysteries of their religion,

...religion; because, there being an infinite distance from God to the Creature, it was, according to them, only the <term>Demons</term> <note>original: "Demons." In the classical context, a *daimon* was a spirit or intermediary being, not necessarily an evil one, bridging the gap between the perfect gods and mortal humans.</note> who could fill this void and, by facilitating communication, transmit the will of the Gods to them. Likewise, the Latins did not reason poorly; for in attributing their mysteries sometimes to the Gods and sometimes to the Demons, these two Principles differed in their hypothesis only as much as a primary cause differs from a secondary one that borrows its power from the first. That is to say, when they traced back to the first cause of their Religion and the source of their Ceremonies, they said that the Gods were their founders and directors; and when they focused on the channels of transmission, they said they were the Demons or the <term>Genii</term> <note>original: "Genies." From the Latin *genius*, a protective spirit or guiding force.</note>. Thus, I pray you not to criticize these expressions, which I shall use interchangeably.

Furthermore, I do not intend at all to delve deeply into this matter; it has been exhausted by a multitude of Authors. My design is only to show you that the Pagans often spoke of their mysteries with little respect, and that if the vulgar <note>The "vulgar" refers to the common people or the uneducated masses, as opposed to the intellectual elite.</note> adored the veils <note>A metaphor for the outward rituals and allegories that hidden the "truth" of the mysteries.</note>, the learned men suspected them violently.

This was because it was imagined that only the Priests had the right, by virtue of the Laws, to consult the good <term>Demons</term> <note>From the Greek *daimon*, referring here to intermediary spirits between gods and mortals, rather than exclusively evil entities.</note>; and that consequently the Magicians—who were merely private individuals without a calling—acted only through illusion, or at most through commerce with evil Demons who asked for nothing better than to give signs of their malice through their ministry.

This is why the Pagans, who held the very name of Magic in horror, gave their mysteries the name of Divination; and in order to establish a more real difference, they changed the various subjects as much as they could and increased the number of types.

In general, the Pagans were great enthusiasts of portents <term>Prodiges: From the Latin "prodigium," these were extraordinary events or "marvels" interpreted as signs of divine will or warnings of future disasters.</term>. No surprising event occurred—Princes could neither be born nor die, and no battle could be won or lost—without the Gods appearing to change the Laws of Nature.

Do not believe, however, that the learned allowed themselves to be caught in this trap; *those who love, fashion dreams for themselves* <note>original: "qui amant ipsi sibi somnia fingunt" — a quote from Virgil's Eclogues (8.108), suggesting that people often invent the very supernatural signs they wish to see.</note>. Cicero, in particular, does not spare them. "Is it," he says, "that this is capable of frightening us, when we are told that certain portents are born of either beasts or men? Regarding this, I will cite only one reason so as not to be too long. It is necessary that everything produced draws its origin from nature; so that one should not believe that if something happens outside of custom, it has happened outside of nature. Therefore, seek the cause of a new and admirable thing if you can find it; if not, be persuaded that nothing can be done without cause. Dispel this error that the novelty of the thing has caused you through the knowledge of Nature; and thus neither earthquakes, nor—"

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of ancient oracles, transitioning from a critique of legendary statues to a balanced investigation into whether or not demons were involved in pagan prophecy. <page-num>126</page-num>

<header># Historical Treatise</header>

It may be true, without it necessarily following that all Oracles were pure impostures. His Critique is very exact, the passages faithfully cited, and the facts he reports are drawn from unsuspicious Authors. However, the difficulty still remains: namely, if the majority of Oracles owed their credit to the artifice of Priests and the credulity of the People, might there not have been some in which the Demon <note>In this context, "the Demon" refers to the devil or fallen spirits, whom many Christian theologians believed were the actual voices behind pagan oracles.</note> had a hand? Might he not have sometimes taken advantage of them to lure the Pagans <term>Payens: Followers of the polytheistic religions of the ancient world.</term> with some illusions <note>original: "prestiges." This refers to supernatural deceptions or "lying wonders" intended to lead people astray.</note> which, although rare, nevertheless seem to have been necessary to keep them in awe? Otherwise, they would have soon been disillusioned, no matter what care the Priests took to veil their ministries and authorize their deceits.

But if there were some Oracles delivered by Demons, it is certain that there were an infinite number of others in which they had no part. This is something the Pagans knew even better than we do. Every day they saw convincing and detailed proof of a thousand defects too coarse to be attributed to Gods or Demons. They were not ignorant of the fact that in things which can be explained naturally, there is not always a need to resort to the supernatural.

<summary>The author argues that while most oracles were likely human frauds perpetrated by priests, one cannot rule out occasional demonic intervention used to maintain the public's religious awe. However, he emphasizes that even ancient people recognized many "miracles" were simply natural occurrences or obvious fakes.</summary>
<keywords>oracles, demons, paganism, priestly fraud, supernatural vs. natural, Fontenelle, religious critique</keywords>

<page-num>130</page-num>
<header>Historical Treatise</header>

God, whose inspirations were not to be neglected. Nothing more was needed. A Temple was built there, and an Oracle was established, along with Priests, a Pythia <note>The High Priestess of the Temple of Apollo at Delphi who delivered the oracles</note>, and Ceremonies.

The vapor <term>exhalaison: a gas or "fume" rising from the earth, which modern geologists suggest may have been ethylene or other intoxicating hydrocarbons</term> that went to the Priestess's head agitated her violently; it was the inspiration of the God seizing her. She spoke nonsensically because the God was battling her faculties; she returned to herself and pronounced the Oracle because the God, having become the Master, spoke through her voice <note>original: "organe," referring to her physical vocal apparatus acting as a tool for the divine</note>.

The force of the vapor was sometimes so violent that it caused the Pythia to die. Plutarch <note>Plutarch (c. 46–120 AD) was a Greek philosopher and a priest at the Temple of Apollo in Delphi</note> provides us with an example of this.

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Obsolescence of Oracles*.</margin>
> *What then happened to the Pythia? She indeed descended into the chasm of the Oracle against her will. But she showed at once that she could no longer endure the vapor, being filled with a malevolent and mute spirit. Finally, being completely disturbed and running toward the door with a horrible and terrifying cry, she threw herself to the ground, such that not only did the travelers flee in fear, but also the High Priest Nicandrus and all the other Priests and religious officials who were present there; however, returning a short time later, they carried her away while she was still delirious; and in fact, she survived for only a few days.*

<margin>
*Cicero, On Divination, book 1.*
*Plutarch, On the Obsolescence of Oracles.*
</margin>
Based on this principle of exhalations <term>éxhalaisons: the vapors rising from the earth previously discussed as the source of the Pythia's trance</term>, Cicero and Plutarch claim to explain why the Oracles have ceased. *It is*, says Cicero, *that this earthly power that stirred the spirit of the Pythia through divine inspiration has vanished over time; just as we see many rivers have dried up, or have taken another course and been diverted elsewhere.* <note>Cicero, *De Divinatione*, Book I, Section 19. The author uses this naturalistic explanation to downplay the supernatural element.</note> But this reason would be extremely weak if one did not add the Enlightenment of Philosophy, whose degrees of perfection were as many milestones in the ruin of the Oracles. <note>The author suggests that human reason and scientific progress, rather than just geological changes, destroyed the credibility of the oracles.</note>

One would only need to examine the location of the places where the Oracles were delivered to agree that these mysteries were nothing but a chain of tricks <note>original: "artifices," suggesting deliberate deception by the priests</note>. The setting was the most convenient in the world for such deception, usually on high mountains bordered by precipices and rocks, shaded by thick forests. One had to undertake long and arduous journeys to reach them, enduring the heat of the Sun and the barrenness of vast plains; this was because the Oracle distanced itself from civil society, which it viewed with suspicion.

How well-conceived all of this was! Those who came to consult it, already biased in its favor and their imaginations filled with the marvelous, felt a renewed sense of fear as they approached the

> She sings dreadful riddles, and bellows from the cave,

Cicero will tell you some very clever things <note>original: "jolies choses," literally "pretty things," but used here with 18th-century irony to mean sharp or witty critiques</note> regarding this in his *Books on <term>Divination: the practice of seeking knowledge of the future or the unknown by supernatural means</term>*.

<margin>Eusebius, Book 4 of *Preparation for the Gospel*.</margin>
Above all, Oenomaus—a Greek philosopher and orator often cited by <term>Eusebius: a 4th-century Christian historian and bishop</term>—attacks the Oracles in the most devastating manner possible, by compiling a precisely detailed catalog of their ambiguities and their falsehoods.

One more reflection, Sir; it will amuse you. The Gods sometimes liked to enjoy themselves with beautiful women. They would demand them, richly adorned, from the hands of their husbands; and in the belief <note>original: "prévention," referring to a deep-seated religious conviction or prejudice</note> of the honor the God was bestowing, the women were sent to him like victims laden with rich gifts.

it is well-established that it was not unheard of,

This single example is worth a thousand, being
reported by a Historian such as Jose-
phus <note>Flavius Josephus (c. 37–100 AD), a Romano-Jewish historian whose account of this scandal is found in *Antiquities of the Jews*, Book 18</note>, who would not have dared to mark
the mysteries of the Pagans with such
infamy—whose sensibilities he
was obliged to handle carefully for the
reasons you know—if the fact
had not been public, and detailed as
he reports it.

<meta>catchword: Comme</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the scandal of Paulina and Mundus proves that Romans were culturally conditioned to believe gods could desire mortal women, a belief the author suggests was even more prevalent in less 'civilized' nations. He asserts the story's credibility by noting that the historian Josephus, despite needing to stay in the good graces of the Romans, could not omit such a well-known public disgrace.</summary>

As you are a man who draws conclusions from everything, I foresee that you will not fail to attribute the birth of most of these Heroes and demigods of Paganism to similar encounters <note>The author is suggesting that "divine" conceptions were actually the result of human trickery or adultery, similar to the scandal of Paulina described on the previous page</note>; and perhaps you will be clever enough to doubt that Philip was the father of Alexander the Great <note>Philip II of Macedon. Ancient rumors suggested his wife Olympias was impregnated by a god, not by Philip</note>. At the very least, you will say, most historians claim he was fathered by Jupiter Hammon <note>Jupiter Ammon: a deity created from the synthesis of the Roman Jupiter and the Egyptian god Amun; Alexander famously visited his oracle at the Siwa Oasis</note>; and that it was for this reason that the Oracle of Delphi ordered Philip to venerate this God.

Make of this, Sir, whatever reflection you wish. For my part, I shall end this letter, which is already too long, by asking you to conclude from everything we have said, that although the Pagans have corrupted the doctrine of Demons <term>Demons: from the Greek 'daimon,' referring to tutelary spirits or lesser deities that act as intermediaries between gods and men, rather than purely evil spirits</term> with many fables, one needs only eyes to see that they have retained various truths from it; that they made a great distinction between Demons and Gods, that they conceived of them only as subordinate agents, and that, at heart, they were not such devout observers of their Mysteries <term>Mysteries: secret religious rituals of the ancient world to which only initiates were admitted</term> that they did not often accuse them of impostures. I remain, etc.

<header>Historical Treatise</header>

## FOURTH LETTER.

->SIR,<-

I am no less surprised by your Letter than you say you were by my previous one. You have nearly placed me among the followers of **Mr. Bekker** <note>Balthasar Bekker (1634–1698) was a Dutch theologian famous for his work *The World Bewitched*, in which he argued against the existence of sorcery and the active influence of the Devil or demons in the physical world—a view considered highly radical and even heretical at the time</note>. You say that you do not think that by thus evading all that is supernatural in the **Divination** <term>Divination: the practice of seeking knowledge of the future or the unknown by supernatural means</term> of the Pagans, and particularly in the **Oracles**, you are refuting yourself. If all the Mysteries of Paganism were nothing but pure artifice and evident impostures where everything was natural, what will become of the operations of **Demons**?

You understand nothing of it, Sir. If you had slightly examined what I wrote to you about it, you would have immediately perceived that my intention is not to say that the Pagans absolutely denied the operations of Demons in their mysteries; but only to show how far most of their **Scholars** were from blindly believing so many little tales, where Demons were always made to intervene without necessity. The most incredulous confessed indeed that there were certain events that they could not reconcile

<meta>catchword: with</meta>

<summary>In this opening to the Fourth Letter, the author defends himself against accusations of radical skepticism (Bekkerism), clarifying that while he exposes the fraud in pagan oracles, he is documenting the historical skepticism of ancient scholars rather than denying the supernatural entirely.</summary>
<keywords>Balthasar Bekker, divination, oracles, Paganism, Demons, skepticism, supernatural, ancient scholars</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his position on ancient skepticism. He argues that while ancient scholars might have accepted the existence of demons in theory, they were highly skeptical of specific religious practices like augury and the Delphic oracles. He uses this to distance himself from the more radical, exhaustive skepticism of Balthasar Bekker.</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>
<header>Of Gods & Demons, etc.</header>

with the ordinary laws of nature, certain facts that exhausted all their enlightenment <note>original: *lumiéres*—referring here to the scholars' intellectual reach or rational understanding</note>. But at heart, they could legitimately doubt the good faith of those who lived only by Oracles.

They did not deny in general the operations of **Demons**, but they had the curiosity to examine whether the entrails of victims, or if the **sacred chickens** <note>The *pulli sacrarii* used in Roman divination; their behavior while eating was interpreted as an omen for military actions</note>, effectively received those influences; whether the **Pythia** <term>Pythia: the high priestess of the Temple of Apollo at Delphi who served as an oracle</term> on her **Tripod** divinely inspired, delivered any different Oracles than they themselves could have produced. They weighed every word, every circumstance; and they concluded by a thousand experiences that they knew at least as much as the Demons, and even **Apollo** himself. And thus, without absolutely denying the operations of Demons, they simply rejected that great mass of impostures and fables upon which the **common people** <note>original: *le vulgaire*—the uneducated masses</note> fed with greed.

How much trouble **Mr. Bekker** would have spared himself, if he had been willing to build upon this Principle! By cutting from his books that useless multitude of stories—selected and detailed to his own advantage—he would have thereby reduced his work to a little less than half. For what is the use of throwing oneself into this forest? How many volumes would one not compose if one wanted to collect all these **little tales**? Is that

the state of the question? The Pagans <note>original: *Payens*—referring here to the ancient Greeks and Romans</note> mocked them; and should we admit them without examination?

But let us see what will result from the reasoning of the Author <note>Likely referring to Balthasar Bekker</note>. One can naturally explain the facts he reports, without being obliged to have the Devil intervene; and consequently, there will be no other instance where he has operated. What an induction! This argument proves nothing; because it proves too much.

But what need is there to examine all these facts to prove the operations of Demons? We have only to follow the path we have traced; it is short and natural; it leads us without detour to an infallible source.

Let us therefore abandon to the criticism of Mr. **Bekker** this infinite number of little stories <note>original: *historiettes*—implying trivial or unreliable anecdotes</note> where the Devil is always made to preside. Let all the peoples of the world have worked in concert—by deceiving themselves—to create an illusion for us. I confess that one could not push liberality <note>original: *liberalité*—meaning generosity in granting a point during an argument</note> further. Also, we are only liberal in order to reduce the question to a simple Principle, stripped of all the incidents that one could bring to bear upon it, to criticize its evidence.

All the nations of the world tell us of Demons: They all agree on the essentials. They are Intelli-

[intelli]gences <note>The word "intelligences" begins on the previous page</note> whose nature is less excellent than that of the Gods, beings who are inferior to them, ministerial agents <term>ministerial agents: intermediaries or spirits who carry out the will of a higher deity</term>, some of whom are good, peaceful, destined to help men, to notify them of the will of the Gods, and to urge them to carry out their commands; while others, on the contrary, are malicious agents, hating men, working only for their ruin, to afflict them, and to defile them with crimes. From where does this unanimous and constant consent of peoples in all times and in all places come?

It comes, Sir <note>original: *Mr.*—an abbreviation for *Monsieur*</note>, from the same Principle from which they have drawn so many truths with which they have clothed their Gods; from the Old Testament which, shining everywhere with the marks of Divinity, so struck the minds of the Pagans <note>original: *Payens*—referring here to the ancient Greeks, Romans, and other non-Christian civilizations</note> that, to make their Gods more venerable, they believed they could do no better than to fashion them after the history it gives us of the Patriarchs <note>The biblical fathers, such as Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob</note>, and to attribute to them its principal features.

I could not, Sir, refuse you the satisfaction you desire, by giving you two or three palpable examples of this conformity. Several great men have shown it clearly. But as you perhaps do not have these kinds of books, and as, moreover, nothing could be more exact or better...

What effect, do you think, was produced by so many Miracles, which were so calamitous to Egypt? They struck the mind powerfully; they left deep impressions there. There was no heart that did not tremble with horror, no memory that did not preserve their recollection, and no age that was not informed of them.

This is why we should not be surprised that the Egyptians, who had been eyewitnesses to so many miraculous events, retained within their mysteries and applied to their false Gods the actions and various circumstances of the lives of these <term>Holy men (Sts. hommes): Specifically referring to Moses and Aaron, whom the author views as historical figures whose deeds were later mythologized by the pagans.</term> who were the leaders of Israel, and whose calling God sealed with so many miracles.

# Historical Treatise on Truths.

You will be no less surprised than I, if you take the trouble to compare Plutarch’s Typhon <note>original: "Tiphon". In Greek accounts of Egyptian myth, Typhon/Set is the antagonist of Osiris.</note> with Moses.

This proper name *Typhon* signifies *inundation* <note>The author likely associates the name with the Greek "typhon" (whirlwind/storm), connecting it to the "inundation" or flooding of the Red Sea.</note>; Moses was the Minister whom God used to inundate Pharaoh and a portion of his People. Typhon was the son of Isaac, who was of the race of Hercules; Moses was descended from the family of Isaac. <note>The author is pointing out how pagan genealogies often conflated biblical figures like Isaac with their own heroes like Hercules to create a hybrid "fabled" history.</note>

Typhon had two sons named *Jerusalem* <note>original: "Hierosolymus"</note> and *Judea* <note>original: "Judæus"</note>. This cannot be applied literally to Moses. But as this great <term>Lawgiver (Legislateur): A traditional title for Moses, referring to his role in delivering the Torah/Law to the Israelites.</term> was the Leader of the Israelites and as he led them to the very borders of Canaan, the promise of which God confirmed through his mouth, and since—by virtue of this promise of God reiterated by Moses—they took possession of Jerusalem and Judea, nothing more was needed for the Pagans (from whom a great many centuries had hidden the exact knowledge of the History of the Patriarchs) to lead them to believe that Jerusalem and Judea were the two sons of their Typhon; that is to say, of Moses.

Typhon had a sister named *Naphté* <note>Nephthys, an Egyptian goddess often paired with Set/Typhon.</note>, very celebrated for her beauty and her victories; from this, it comes that the Pagans made her a Goddess and placed her among the Stars. Miriam <note>original: "Marie"</note>, the sister of Moses

In ancient Egyptian symbolism, red was the color associated with Set/Typhon, often representing the desert or chaos.</note>, which is to say, handsome, according to the style of the Orientals; likewise, Moses was perfectly beautiful, "fair in the sight of God" <note>original Greek: ἀστεῖος τῷ Θεῷ (*asteios tō Theō*). Typhon performed several wonders near the Nile and the sea. This is why the Egyptians hated this Element <note>The author refers to water/the sea as an "element."</note>, upon which Typhon had carried out his cruel undertakings. Likewise, Moses specifically caused his miracles to shine forth upon this river and upon the Red Sea, which God employed to punish this rebellious and hardened People.

The Egyptians worshipped Typhon as a malevolent God; they served him so that he would not afflict them with new misfortunes. This suits Moses perfectly well, as he had made himself formidable to this People by the <term>plagues (playës): The ten miraculous disasters brought upon Egypt to secure the release of the Israelites.</term> he had inflicted upon them.

Typhon made use of certain evil and harmful animals to afflict Egypt; Moses employed frogs, lice, etc.

<margin>Exodus 12:12</margin>

Above all, it is one of the most remarkable things from Antiquity: the fable that Plutarch <note>Plutarch (c. 46 – 119 AD) was a Greek philosopher whose essay *On Isis and Osiris* is the primary source for this myth.</note> relates concerning the chest of Osiris thrown into the Nile, and the various circumstances accompanying it. It is true that he confuses matters there, transposing persons and names by applying all the features of this Sacred History not to Typhon—that is to say, to Moses—but to Osiris himself, the King of Egypt. This is an effect of the malice of the ancient Pagans and the ignorance of the moderns. But ultimately, the history matches that of Moses in substance. Let us untangle its features.

Who could doubt that this entire history was drawn from that of Moses? Take the trouble, Sir, to clarify this for yourself by comparing it with the first verses of the second chapter of the Book of Exodus.

One might observe here that the reason why the wisdom of the Egyptians was so famous and so curiously sought after by all peoples, stems from all those miracles that God displayed there through the ministry of His servants. the Books of Moses and the other sacred Writers.

It would be useless to prove that the Egyptians, the Chaldeans, and the Phoenicians were informed of them. The proximity and the connections that these Peoples had with the Israelites communicated to them most of the Historical events.

Beyond that, it is impossible that the exactness of these idolatrous Peoples in recording in their public Archives those acts that had some relation to the History of their country, did not lead them to include the principal facts regarding the Israelites.

The Theology and Philosophy of Egypt contributed considerably to this. They discovered there a thousand defective things which had clearly been drawn from elsewhere. It was necessary to learn about them more exactly, and consequently to consult Sacred history.

<header>Of Gods and Demons, etc. 169</header>

Jewish <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page, referring to the "Jewish History."</note>, having even conferred with the Jews whose wisdom they admired, borrowed several truths from them, as well as from the Egyptians and the Phoenicians, to make them the subject of their Mythology.

This will seem even more natural to you if you add to this means of knowledge that of the dispersal of the Canaanites, who after having been subdued by Joshua, first threw themselves onto the coasts of Phoenicia, and then spread along the Mediterranean, from where they divided into various colonies, which went to establish themselves in various places in Europe, Asia, and Africa.

This passage from *Procopius* <note>Procopius of Caesarea was a prominent 6th-century Byzantine historian.</note> is too explicit to omit. *All this country,* he says, *which extends from Sidon to Egypt, was formerly called Phoenicia.* Those who have written the History of the *Phoenicians* report that in the past a single King ruled there. *The Girgashites, the Jebusites, and other peoples <note>These are groups mentioned in the Hebrew Bible as inhabitants of Canaan before the Israelite conquest.</note> inhabited the limits of that country. Their language is semi-Phoenician. Among others, they built in Numidia the city of Tangier, very—*

> WE HAVE FLED FROM BEFORE THE FACE OF THAT ROBBER, JOSHUA SON OF NUN. <note>original: "NOUS AVONS FUI DE DEVANT LA FACE DE CE VOLEUR JOSUE FILS DE NUN." This famous inscription is cited by the historian Procopius of Caesarea to support the claim that the Canaanites fled to North Africa to escape the Israelite conquest.</note>

It is certain that these peoples—being well-informed of the history of the Patriarchs <note>The biblical figures like Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob.</note> who had stayed among them, of the wonders God had performed for the Israelites in Egypt, and of the miraculous victories they had just won over them—it is certain, I say, that they spread these histories everywhere, and taught them particularly to the Greeks, among whom they made their homes.

Above all, one must attribute these ideas that the Pagans held regarding the history of Moses to the Jews of the Ten Tribes <note>The "Lost Ten Tribes" of the Northern Kingdom of Israel, who were exiled by the Neo-Assyrian Empire around 722 BCE.</note> who were dispersed throughout several parts of the world. The Assyrians, to whom they were subjected, transported them to distant lands and repopulated the Jews' land with other peoples. They forced them to move beyond Media <note>An ancient region of north-western Iran.</note>.

These Jews settled among the Colchians <note>Inhabitants of Colchis, an ancient region on the eastern coast of the Black Sea, in modern-day Georgia.</note> and the Tartars <note>A historical term used by Europeans to describe various nomadic groups of Central and Northern Asia.</note> shortly after their captivity in Assyria. Now, the commerce that the Chinese and neighboring peoples had with the Tartars—who had learned various truths from the Books of Moses via the Jews—caused these stories to spread among those nations as well.

I do not speak at all of the captivity of the Jews in Babylon; because it cannot pass for a true dispersion, as it lasted only 70 years; and thus it was neither general enough nor of a long enough duration to spread and establish the history of the Jewish nation among other peoples.

Beyond these reasons, what is remarkable regarding the Greeks, and what shows that they have read the *Books of Moses*, is that long before the version of the Septuagint <note>The earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible, traditionally dated to the 3rd century BCE.</note>, even before Alexander the Great, the Law of Moses and the history of the Israelites' departure from Egypt had been translated into Greek. It is Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 CE), a historian and bishop often called the "Father of Church History."</note> who informs us of this on the testimony of Aristobulus, a Jewish Peripatetic <note>A follower of the philosophy of Aristotle.</note>; a passage which he took from his first book to Philometor <note>Ptolemy VI Philometor, King of Egypt (reigned 180–145 BCE).</note>.

# Of Gods and of Demons, etc.

[Poe]ts have attributed to him <note>Referring to the serpent.</note> an infinity of surprising things; if all travelers have observed a thousand marvelous experiences of it, one must either deny their observations, or admit that all they tell us of serpents surpasses the forces of nature.

But, from whichever side one turns, I will infer first, that if one adds faith to the accounts of so many eye-witnesses, it will also be necessary to recognize that all this "Marvelous" quality they have observed in the use that idolaters <note>A term used by historical Christian authors to describe those who worshiped entities other than the God of the Bible.</note> make of serpents in their **Magic** & their **enchantments**, must flow from some cause other than nature, which cannot always play with the superstition of man against the ordinary course of its laws. It must also be admitted that this cause can only be an evil and seductive agent; and consequently the **Devil**.

If, in the second place, one argues against all these experiences as being false, it will be to leave one difficulty only to enter another. Nature has given nothing to serpents more excellent than to other animals. There are several that have much more exquisite qualities; even the serpent seems to have been formed only to destroy other animals: And it is not without a particular **Providence** <note>The protective care of God or of nature as a spiritual power.</note> that it walks on its belly, that its

# Historical Treatise

[...movements] are slow and languid. If it had as much agility and speed in its movement as it has fury and venom, it would have soon made a desert of the earth.

It is well known that a blind **superstition** can also produce a bizarre **cult**. We have seen entire peoples offering incense to the vilest of creatures. But it is also known that there is a great difference here. If one nation consecrated a certain species of animals, another would have rejected it. There would have been as much diversity as there were peoples. On the contrary, the **worship of serpents** has been constant and universal. It is as if a spell were cast over all the peoples that fixated them upon it. Not only do the **Poets**, **Philosophers**, **Naturalists**, and **Historians** of antiquity inform us of this; but what is particularly notable is that all those peoples of the **Indies** <note>In this period, "Indies" was a broad term referring to South and Southeast Asia, as well as the Americas ("West Indies").</note> who have been discovered in these recent centuries were also infatuated with it.

But it would be a waste of time to stop to report and examine all the passages of the **Old Testament** <note>Original: "V. T." (*Vieux Testament*).</note> where the **Devil** is mentioned, and from which the Pagans drew their doctrine of **Demons**. **Mr. Bekker** <note>Balthasar Bekker (1634–1698), a Dutch theologian whose book *The Enchanted World* argued that the Devil has no power or presence in the physical world, a highly controversial view at the time.</note> does not deny that the Jews were instructed in the existence of **Angels** and of **Satan**. He even extends his generosity so far as to acknowledge that they are formally spoken of in the **Old Testament**.

The continuous style, he adds, of the Word of God, gives us enough to understand that there are *Angels* and *Devils*.

Mr. Bekker has not noticed that by this admission he entirely ruins his System <note>Bekker's "system" argued that because God is all-powerful, he would not allow spirits like the Devil to interfere in the physical world; therefore, any belief in such influence was mere superstition.</note>. For however little the O. T. <note>Original: "V. T." (*Vieux Testament*), referring here to the Old Testament.</note> gives us to understand that there are *Angels* and *Devils*, by that very fact it invincibly establishes the operations of these spirits. The consequence seems strange to you at first. The Pagans, you will say, could have learned of the existence of *Demons* from the *Jews* scattered through *Paganism* with the *Bible* which also makes mention of *Angels*—that is to say, of their existence; but if they inferred their operations from it, they did so against the sentiment of the ancient Jews through a pure effect of superstition, which forges a thousand Grotesques <note>In this context, "Grotesques" refers to absurd, distorted, or fantastical religious fictions.</note>. # Historical Treatise
### 182

...[your] exception is null, and that my argument is very natural and entirely conclusive.

I would have wished that you had noted for me those passages of the O.T. <note>Original: "V. T." stands for *Vieux Testament*, the Old Testament.</note> which clearly establish the existence of **Demons**, without one being able to infer their operations. I have examined them with all possible diligence, without finding a single one that does not destroy your exception. The ideas of existence and operation are so joined there, and as if merged, that they form, so to speak, but one and the same notion. One cannot separate them without destroying the rules of common sense and without making the Holy Spirit <note>Original: "St. Esprit."</note> absurd; which is a blasphemy.

Indeed, it is a remarkable thing that the Sacred Writers of the O.T. only speak to us of the existence of **Demons** in relation to their operations, and only teach it to us by presupposing it through the seduction, hatred, calumnies, enmity, fury, enchantments etc. with which God allows them to afflict men. If therefore the Pagans learned from the Bible—that is to say, from the O.T.—the existence of **Demons**, as Mr. Bekker <note>Balthasar Bekker (1634–1698) was a Dutch theologian whose book *The World Bewitched* argued that since God is all-powerful, the Devil cannot actually interfere in human affairs.</note> acknowledges, they would also have learned of their operations there, since it teaches the one only in relation to the other. I am, etc.

# Historical Treatise

<margin>Book 2. Page 43.</margin>
...[government of things which could] be of the greatest importance; he openly contradicts himself a line later by saying that these spirits were, according to the **Pagans**, **Beings** of a nature more perfect than bodies, which lack the spirit required for the government of anything of importance. I could not pass over this objection without making a second reflection. It is that, far from this fertility of the **Philosophers** in producing errors having engendered the operations of **Demons**, it should instead have banished them from the world. Why would these people, immersed in the delights of the age and brutalized by their sensualities, have imagined these objects of terror? *Let us eat and drink, for tomorrow we die* <note>A common biblical and epicurean sentiment, referencing Isaiah 22:13 and 1 Corinthians 15:32.</note>; let us have a good time and joyfully taste the pleasure of life. # Of Gods and Demons, etc.

...communicate. It is well known that the stars have their own light, and that the light of the moon is borrowed and reflected; but, in relation to us, she is a great **Luminary** <note>original: "Luminaire"; referring to the description of the Sun and Moon in Genesis 1:16.</note>, because she transmits more light to us than all the stars combined.

One could also observe the same thing regarding that famous question: namely, whether the **Holy Spirit** <note>original: "St. Esprit"</note> does not speak in the manner of the common people <note>original: "le vulgaire"; meaning the uneducated masses rather than scholars.</note>, when He posits in several places the movement of the sun. The **Cartesians** <note>Followers of the philosophy of René Descartes, who advocated for a sun-centered universe.</note> have found no other way to defend their hypothesis from the attacks of the **Theologians**. But, it seems that it would not be impossible to reconcile their view with these passages;

for by the explanation that **Descartes** gives of the nature of movement, in the second part of his *Principles* <note>Referring to *Principia Philosophiae* (1644), Descartes' foundational work on physics and metaphysics.</note>, he clearly teaches that <term>translation</term> <note>original: "translation"; in Cartesian physics, this refers to the physical transport of a body from the vicinity of those that touch it.</note>—by which a certain body moves near others, which are considered to be at rest—is only a *mode*; and not something self-subsisting, just as shape is the mode of the shaped thing; so that movement and rest are but two different modes.

From this it follows that everything real and positive in the bodies that move must also be found in the others which are contiguous to them, which are nevertheless considered entirely at rest; and consequently this...

<meta>catchword: slow</meta>

the <term>translation</term> <note>original: "translation"; in Cartesian physics, this refers to the physical displacement or transport of a body relative to the objects immediately touching it.</note> of a body from the proximity of another that one regards as fixed, is reciprocal and common to them both.

And thus, by applying this Principle to the movement of the earth and the rest of the **Sun**, one could say that since this movement is only a relative mode, it is common and reciprocal to them; one cannot move without the other—which is assumed to be at rest—also participating in its movement. The reason for this is that the sun, which is considered immobile, changes its proximity no less than the earth which moves around the Sun. In this sense, **Virgil** would have been less a Poet than a **Cartesian Philosopher**.

perception, it would be an absurdity to claim to find any properties <note>In 17th-century philosophy, "affections" (properties or attributes) are the characteristics that belong to a substance.</note> there. Thus, I can set limits to extension <note>The term "extension" (étenduë) refers to the physical world or anything that takes up space, a key concept in Cartesian philosophy.</note> without being obliged to establish any void in nature, or having the clarity of my idea obscured; for I can just as easily say that there is nothing after this vast extension as I can say there was nothing before creation. These two things, if examined without passion, share the same concepts. And as for the common objection—that the mind cannot conceive of an extension bounded by nothing—it is because nothingness provides no ideas of itself, but only in opposition to Being.

If, therefore, **Demons** are pure nothingness—considering them as the common people have conceived them—it is impossible for them to have had the slightest prejudice <note>The author uses "prejudice" (préjugé) in the sense of a "pre-judgment"—a belief formed before the mind has fully analyzed the facts.</note> regarding them; because a prejudice, by definition, requires a subject that provides the understanding with some ideas. When these ideas are not sufficiently developed, the **will** cannot make a true judgment; and when the will decides anyway, that is what we call a prejudice.

My **Minor Premise** <note>In a formal logical argument (a syllogism), the Minor Premise is the second statement that applies the general rule of the Major Premise to a specific case.</note> is evident. If **Demons** had not been known through the path of operation <note>The author uses "operation" to mean the physical actions or manifestations of demons in the world.</note>, they would have been pure nothingness to the **Pagans**. But, it is said, they <note>Referring to the Reformers like Luther and Calvin.</note> gave it almost no thought? *Almost none?* That means they must have had some small thought about it. If that is the case, nothing more was needed for our **Reformers** to discover the poison of this error. In a matter of such consequence, a tiny spark of light is enough to enlighten the mind. This belief contains so many absurdities and impieties that they should strike the mind immediately.

If **Calvin** <note>John Calvin (1509–1564), the French theologian and central figure of the Protestant Reformation.</note> had given it even a little thought, he would not have hesitated to root out these abominable errors. Indeed, his own doctrine naturally led him to do so; he would have thus avoided a manifest contradiction. For while he considered God the sole author and sole arbiter of all things, he nevertheless allows—in **Mr. Bekker's** <note>Balthasar Bekker (1634–1698), whose book *The Enchanted World* argued that if God is all-powerful, then demons cannot have power over the world.</note> hypothesis—a competitor in the government of the Universe who nullifies the designs of **Providence** and grace.

"Steady now," some might say? Our Reformers had their reasons for tolerating this error. It was necessary to paint the **Antichrist** <note>A biblical figure representing the ultimate enemy of Christ; the Reformers frequently used this term to refer to the Papacy.</note> in the ugliest possible colors to inspire as much loathing as possible.

# Of Gods and Demons, etc.

<page-num>227</page-num>

And with what darker strokes could one better represent him, than by applying to the Pope those words of St. Paul: <margin>2 Thessalonians Chapter 2, Verse 9.</margin> *whose coming is according to the working of Satan, with all power and signs and lying wonders?* <note>original: "Ja venuë est selon l'efficace de Satan..." The author is quoting the Bible to show how the Reformers linked the Pope's authority to demonic deception.</note> But did they have no other reasons to disabuse the people? Was the idolatry of this Religion not convincing proof of its falsehood, without employing such a detestable means?

But why make the Devil and the Pope two brothers? Why not make them one and the same person? **Mr. BEKKER** prompts this thought in us by saying that <margin>Book 2, Page 301.</margin> *he who boasts of being the successor of St. Peter should not take offense if one gives him the same name our Lord gave to that Apostle.* <note>Referring to the Gospel of Matthew (16:23), where Jesus says to Peter, "Get thee behind me, Satan." Bekker is using a pun to suggest the Pope, as Peter's successor, could be called Satan.</note> What a terrible prejudice it would have been to personally attribute to the Pope all these names, passages, descriptions, and horrific ideas under which the Devil has been conceived until now! It would have been a very easy thing to do. If one explains these terms—Satan, Devil, Demons—as "Adversary," "Slanderer," or "evil thoughts," <note>Bekker argued that the Bible often uses "Satan" or "Devil" as metaphors for human enemies or wicked impulses rather than literal spirits.</note> there you have the natural portrait of the **Antichrist**. After that, the Pope would not have dared to appear. He would have been only too happy if he had been permitted to confine himself to some monastery! Farewell, Sir. I am, etc.

->THE END.<-

<summary>The author concludes the critique by suggesting that if the Reformers had followed Balthasar Bekker's logic, they could have more effectively dismantled the Papacy by reinterpreting "Demons" as metaphors for their political and religious adversaries.</summary>
<keywords>Balthasar Bekker, Antichrist, Papacy, St. Paul, Satan, Protestant Reformation, polemics, biblical interpretation</keywords>
